Equal-split misallocation self-corrects: over-supported contacts separate and the dual decays toward zero in one update; under-supported ones sink slightly and ramp normally from a far closer start. Tagged `vbd::lambda_seed` (non-zero duration, visible unlike `asleep_skip`). **Default OFF deliberately**: the GPU parity campaign is actively bisecting a DX12 seed with the CPU as reference — a default-on trajectory change would move their reference mid-hunt; flipping the default is its own decision after measurement. The setting gives same-binary A/B, which also solves the before/after problem. **The round**: 16k seed-off baseline ×1, seed-on ×2 (determinism), success = settle frame collapses (currently f277) with stands/0-shed/apex-drop sane; the null test is the drop scenario seed-on vs seed-off — impact contacts fail the approach gate, so the trajectories must be bit-identical, proving the gate; pile seed-on as a secondary observation (spawns near-rest, gets seeded).

**MEASURED 2026-08-13 — REFUTED. The equal-split pre-seed is strictly harmful at every tested scale; never re-enable as-is.** The gate architecture itself worked perfectly: the `lambda_seed` tag fired in exactly one frame (spawn) on the drop scenario and never at impact, and the drop's resting box visibly settled cheaper (p95 1.66 → 0.50 ms) — seeding is fine at own-weight load scale, the regime that never needed help. At structural scale it detonates: **pyr50** seeded churns at 1–2.8 m/s through f240 with every block in motion, sheds 3, and freezes only at f457 against f231 unseeded — settle time doubled. **16k** seeded explodes outright: max speed 22.4 m/s by frame 5 (baseline 1.15), pinned at the 15 m/s clamp from f60, 8,320 of 16,291 bodies displaced past 2 m by f599. The mechanism is arithmetic, not tuning: per-contact allocation error under equal split scales with the *absolute* column load — kilonewtons at 180 rows — and the erroneous net force acts in the primal immediately, under frozen Jacobians, before the dual can relax; the per-body Newton step saturates its 0.1 m clamp, and BDF1 converts that clamped displacement into 0.1 × 240 ≈ **24 m/s of derived velocity per substep** — the observed 22.4 is this signature, not chaos. The self-correction argument (over-supported contacts separate, dual decays toward zero) is true but arrives exactly one substep after the kick. Conclusion for the ledger: any workable pre-seed must bound per-contact allocation error to roughly own-weight scale, which equal split structurally cannot at depth — and computing the *correct* static distribution is the force-balance problem itself, i.e. the job of a coarse-level static solve. **The pre-seed lead therefore folds into the multigrid lead** rather than standing alone: a coarse-grid equilibration IS the principled version of this idea. Code disposition: **DELETED same day** — the pass, setting, and plumbing are gone (no diagnostic value, unlike the ordered sweep); the `replayed` bit-2 fresh-contact provenance and the `& 1u` store mask stay, general-purpose. Post-mortem detail from the exploded run's profile: the pass re-fired every frame at ~10 ms because the explosion kept minting fresh near-rest contacts — any future variant needs a stricter once-per-spawn trigger, recorded here so the multigrid-era version inherits it. Separate attribution note from the same txts: the "settle frames doubled on this tree" alarm (p95 851 → 1550) is mostly ambient contention — every tag inflated uniformly 15–35% while the frame rose 82%, and concurrent agent builds were running; a genuine shapes-cost check needs one naturally-cold round, low priority.
2. **Global adaptive iteration budget** driven by the `max_violation` the dual update already returns — spend 40 while violation is high, decay toward 15 as it falls. CAUTION: cousin of the refuted per-contact early exit; if ever tried, gate on the global violation trend only, and hold it to the standing verdicts (does 120 stand, drift, settle invariants).
3. **Multigrid on the contact graph** — the only exponent-changer: coarsening moves load globally per cycle, O(log rows) transport against the dual update's O(rows). See lever 5's closing note. Research-scale; park until tall-stacks-at-low-iterations is a real content need.
4. **Finer coloring for settle width** — settle frames run ~45–50% parallel efficiency (`gs_color_iter` 4.6 s CPU-sum inside ~600 ms wall on 16 workers); color-group serialization bounds the width. Modest, independent, touches no convergence math.

**Parked pending the policy decision:** the `emit` narrow shave (~19–21 ms serial, realistic ceiling about half) and sleeping-subgraph persistence (2b — the per-island generalization of 2a for the partially-asleep gameplay case, the last big CPU project). Both were justified by "CPU asleep frames at 16k scale are the endgame"; if settling-heavy scenes become the GPU's job under the policy, that justification thins to nothing.

**Standing scheduled item:** divergence 5, isotropic inertia — fix after Phase 1, full record in the divergences section.

## The harness

**Pyramid.** `pyramid_cpu` / `pyramid_gpu`, scene `Pyramid`, headless, `warmup_frames = 0`. Size is a user-scoped setting so it sweeps without a rebuild:

```
Sandbox.exe --engine-bench-scenario pyramid_cpu --no-engine-persist-settings \
  --engine-setting "Dev Spawn.pyramid.base_count=50" \
  --engine-bench-state-dump-out pyr50.dump
```

The scene owns a floor and camera; `dev_spawn` spawns the blocks when the scene becomes active, so the setting drives the size on every path. F9 and the Dev Spawn menu button spawn one into any scene. Blocks are 1 m, 20 kg, one box deep, each row offset 0.5 m so every block straddles a seam.

**`pyramid16k_cpu`** is the 16k configuration as a first-class scenario: it pins `Dev Spawn.pyramid.base_count=180` and `Physics.solver_iterations=40` through `scenario::info::settings`, which rides the same override mechanism as `--engine-setting` (scenario pins are prepended, so explicit command-line settings still win). `pyramid_cpu` stays at base 50 deliberately — it is the reference every baseline table in this document was measured against, and 16k at the default 15 iterations is a guaranteed collapse.

**Per-frame state dump.** `--engine-bench-state-dump-out` writes one `state_dump_record` per body per measured frame. 12-byte header, then 40-byte records: owner u64, frame u32, position 3×f32 at +12, velocity 3×f32 at +24. Reconstruct the pyramid layout from the first fully-populated frame: `row = round(y - 0.5)`, `col = round(x + (base - row - 1) * 0.5)`.

Body count **varies per frame** on the scenario path, because the blocks spawn a frame after settle — group records by frame rather than assuming a fixed stride.

**Displacement is not penetration.** A block that slides off the side and lands on the ground reads as tens of metres of "sink". Use inter-row mean-y gaps for penetration and apex height for structural integrity.

**Comparator.** `--compare-states-a/-b` with `--compare-states-threshold`, `--compare-states-focus-frame/-bodies/-window`, and `--compare-states-align`. `--scan-states` with `--scan-states-speed` scans a *single* dump for implausible speeds — the right instrument for "does it explode", where drift is not, because drift conflates a blowup with ordinary nondeterminism.

**Parity ladder**, code-built, no assets: `ParityDrop` (2 bodies), `ParityPair` (3), `ParityStack` (9), `ParityShapes` (16), `ParityCluster` (28), `ParityHeap`, `ParityMound`, `ParityPile` (217), `ParityOverlap` (217, interpenetrating at spawn). Each has `cpu`/`gpu` scenarios sharing the scene.

**`ParityShapes` is the coverage rung, not a scale rung — added 2026-08-13 with the GPU shape-kind work.** Every other scene on the ladder is boxes, which is why the GPU narrow phase being box-only survived undetected: a sphere collided as a cube and nothing in the harness could see it. `ParityShapes` is six well-separated groups, one per shape pair, so a divergence localizes to a single narrow-phase path: box-box (box on static box), box-sphere (sphere on a static box, which takes the 4-point ring branch), box-capsule (capsule laid along Z on a widened static box), sphere-sphere (sphere cradled between two static spheres), sphere-capsule (sphere cradled between two parallel static capsules), capsule-capsule (capsule laid along X across two static capsules). Cradles rather than stacked pairs because a sphere balanced on a sphere is a knife-edge equilibrium, and a baseline scene must not amplify ULP noise into a false alarm; every contact here is a stable two-point rest that persists for the whole run, so warm-starting and sticking get exercised too. The box-capsule support is 1.6 m wide on purpose — at 1.0 m the capsule's endpoints land exactly on the face edges, which is precisely the boundary case where two backends can legitimately pick different features. Gate entry starts at `report`/`report`; promote to `require` only after a first round establishes the baseline, since asserting a number nobody has measured is not a gate.

## The standing parity invariant, and the next goal (2026-08-13)

**Read this before quoting a gate result, because the gate asserts two different things and they are routinely confused.** A *determinism* check runs one configuration N times and demands a single world-state hash: it proves that CPU-alone, Vulkan-alone, and DX12-alone each reproduce themselves. An *identity* check compares the CPU hash against the GPU hash. **A green determinism board does not mean the GPU matches the CPU**, and the current state is close to the opposite of that reading: identity is only declared on three ladder entries at all — `drop`, `pair`, and `shapes` — and every identity check that ran in the 2026-08-13 round **failed**, so **no scenario currently has CPU↔GPU bit-identity**. Those failures are the pre-existing CPU-side re-baseline from the parallel physics work (shapes COM / constants sweep / pile-sleep), not a GPU regression, and they remain an open bisect owned outside this campaign; the tell that it is CPU-side is that **both GPU backends agree with each other to the bit on `drop`** (`0xa9161639ef868c2c`) while the CPU sits elsewhere. On `pair` the two backends also differ from each other, which is the long-documented stable DXIL-vs-SPIR-V FMA-contraction delta, not a defect.

**What is now invariant, checked every iteration — and one promotion that had to be taken back the same hour.** With the readback race fixed and the evidence in hand (20 DX12 mound pairs at trace grade plus a full both-backend gate round), the determinism checks that held were promoted from `report` to `require` in `scripts/parity_gate.py`, then **validated by two further gate rounds before the promotion was allowed to stand** — which is the step that earns the word "invariant", because asserting a tier off a single round is how a gate acquires false alarms. Across three rounds (six runs per configuration) **`stack`, `cluster`, `heap`, `mound`, and `pyramid` held on both backends**, including `heap`, which had been report-only precisely because of a documented 1-in-7 impact-window escape; those are now `require` alongside `drop` and `pair`, so any future loss of run-to-run reproducibility on them is a hard gate failure on either backend. **`pile` was promoted and then demoted, because it failed the validation it was given**: `parity_pile_gpu` on DX12 produced two distinct hashes in one of the three rounds (`0x8c31acd3598b8ae5` vs `0xfc2e7e3a38f71917`) while holding in the other two, so it is back at `report`/`report`. Vulkan `pile` held 6 of 6 runs and is the leading promotion candidate, but requiring it alone on a scene whose sibling backend demonstrably varies would invite exactly the spurious red this validation exists to prevent — promote it only after more rounds, and treat the variance as scene-driven until something proves it backend-specific. **The open nondeterminism class is therefore `overlap`, `stress`, and `pile` on DX12**, all still `report`. That list also refutes the hypothesis floated earlier in this document that one backend-agnostic CPU race would explain them: they survived a change that demonstrably killed the mound class at trace grade, so they are a separate class and must be hunted on their own terms (`overlap` is the interpenetration path, never in the proven set; `stress` is the 1062-body mid-run-spawn reseed seam, where the deliberate CPU-wins reseed contract still lets mirrored state reach the solver; `pile` is 217 bodies in sustained chaotic contact, the historical home of the "DX12 2/129 pile residue"). `shapes` stays `report` and is excluded from routine runs until its GPU hang is fixed, since it burns two 600 s timeouts per backend.

**SHIPPED 2026-08-13 night — the positional check is now in the gate and the gate is green.** `parity_gate.py` grew a third check kind beside determinism and identity: `positional`, which runs the cpu and gpu scenarios with `--engine-bench-state-dump-out`, drives the existing comparator, and asserts the **final-frame** max drift against a per-scenario budget (peak drift is printed alongside but deliberately not asserted — the transient during settling is large and legitimate, e.g. `stack` peaks at 414 mm on its way to 0.465 mm). Budgets are set only where a number has actually been measured, with roughly 3–4× headroom over the observed value: `drop` and `pair` **0.010 mm**, `stack` **2.000 mm**, `pyramid` **5.000 mm**, all `require` on both backends. Per the two-regimes rule the chaotic scenes carry **no positional check at all** — asserting per-body distance on `cluster`/`heap`/`mound`/`pile`/`overlap` would be gating a Lyapunov exponent, and their ensemble-invariant scoring is the next piece of work. Result: **24 checks, 0 required failures** — drop 0.000 mm, pair 0.000 mm, stack 0.465 mm, pyramid 1.722/1.736 mm, every one inside budget on both backends.

**`pair` bit-identity demoted `require` → `report`, with the evidence for why it is not achievable.** After the iteration fix, `pair`'s residual is **14.9 nm on Vulkan and 29.8–59.6 nm on DX12** — about 1 ULP — and, decisively, **the two backends differ from each other as well as from the CPU**. That is the signature of codegen-level float association and FMA contraction across three different compilers (host x86, DXIL, SPIR-V), not a semantic gap: the iteration-budget divergence, which *was* semantic, showed up **identically** on both backends (`stack` was 0.004190 m on each). `drop` — the simpler scene — does reach exact three-way bit-identity, which is what proves the semantics are right and isolates `pair`'s residual to arithmetic. Its positional check passes at 0.000 mm, which is the criterion that actually expresses "the physics agrees". **Use that discriminator generally: a divergence that is identical on both backends is a semantics bug worth chasing; one that differs per backend is arithmetic, and belongs under a tolerance rather than a hash.**

**The next goal: positional parity with the CPU solver.** Hash identity is the wrong instrument for it and cannot be made to work beyond the trivially-ordered scenes. A colored GPU solver and a serial CPU solver differ in contact ordering, reduction order, and FMA contraction, so their final bits differ for any scene with real contact structure even when the physics agrees to microns — which is exactly why identity was only ever asserted on `drop`, `pair`, and `shapes`. The goal is therefore a **tolerance-based positional check**: for each ladder scenario, run CPU and GPU with state dumps and assert that final per-body position (and now orientation) agreement stays inside a per-scenario budget. The machinery already exists and only needs wiring into the gate — `bench_config::state_dump_out` records a per-frame `state_dump_record` (v2, 64-byte records, which **does** carry `orientation` and `angular_velocity`, so an older "the dump has no orientation" note elsewhere in this document is stale), and `Sandbox/Main.cpp` already implements the comparator (`--compare-states-a/-b`, `--compare-states-threshold`, `--compare-states-align`, plus the focus-frame/worst-body reporting) which prints per-frame max drift and the worst offending body. The work is: a new gate check kind that drives both runs and the comparator, a per-scenario tolerance table, and the usual `report`-first-then-promote discipline — **do not assert a tolerance nobody has measured**. Three traps carry over and must be respected by whoever builds it: the two regimes rule below still applies, so settling scenes (`drop`, `pair`, `stack`, `shapes`, pyramid) get tight per-body budgets while the chaotic scenes (`cluster`, `heap`, `pile`, `overlap`) must be scored on ensemble invariants rather than per-body distance, or the check becomes a Lyapunov detector; the GPU dump lags the sim and records each simulation frame twice before skipping, which `--compare-states-align` compensates for but does not fix, so the alignment argument is mandatory and its value must be justified per scenario; and a `--engine-bench-scene` run without a scenario writes `owner 0` with zero velocity for every CPU record, so positional runs must go through the `cpu`/`gpu` scenario pair, never the scene.

### Positional parity: the baseline, and the divergence it exposed (2026-08-13 night)

**The measurement, built from parts that already existed.** `bench_config::state_dump_out` (→ `--engine-bench-state-dump-out`) plus the comparator already in `Sandbox/Main.cpp` gives per-frame max positional drift and the worst body; running the cpu scenario against each gpu backend and reading the final-frame row is the whole instrument. First ladder-wide baseline, final-frame CPU↔GPU drift:

| scene | Vulkan | DX12 | regime |
|---|---|---|---|
| drop | **0.000000 m** | **0.000000 m** | settling |
| pair | **0.000000 m** | **0.000000 m** | settling |
| stack | 0.004190 m | 0.004190 m | settling |
| pyramid | 0.008093 m | 0.008086 m | settling |
| cluster | 0.024629 m | 0.024728 m | chaotic |
| heap | 0.030432 m | 0.049732 m | chaotic |
| mound | 3.116 m | 4.074 m | chaotic |
| pile | 3.745 m | 3.268 m | chaotic |
| overlap | 9.485 m | 13.258 m | chaotic |

Read it through the two-regimes rule below: the settling scenes are the signal, and the metre-scale chaotic numbers are Lyapunov amplification, not a parity defect — those must be scored on ensemble invariants, which is why the positional gate check must never assert per-body distance on `cluster`/`heap`/`mound`/`pile`/`overlap`. Note also how tightly the two backends agree with each other (`stack` identical to the micron, `pyramid` within 7 µm), which is what makes the settling rows a clean CPU-vs-GPU measurement rather than a GPU noise measurement.

**A finding that localised the "required" identity failures — and a correction, because the first conclusion drawn from it was wrong.** Comparing every dumped field at `drop`'s final frame, CPU and GPU differed in **exactly one component of one body — `pos.y` by 44.7 nanometres** (`0.249499977` vs `0.249500021`, ~2 ULP at that magnitude); x/z, all velocities, orientation, and angular velocity were bit-identical. The conclusion drawn at the time — that the identity failures were irreducible float-representation noise and should be deprioritised — **was wrong, and is retracted**: that 44.7 nm was the visible tip of the iteration-budget divergence described below, and the moment the GPU got the CPU's adaptive loop, **`drop` identity began passing on both backends with CPU, Vulkan, and DX12 all landing the same hash `0x14f82f0ca02de8d7`**. The lesson worth keeping is the diagnostic one, not the dismissal: a hash mismatch says nothing about *magnitude*, so always localise it field-by-field before deciding whether it matters — but a nanometre-scale residual can still be a real semantic gap rather than noise, and "it's only ULP" is a hypothesis to test, not a reason to stop. What remains true is the criticism of the instrument: `world_state_hash` serialises **every field of the transform *and* motion components**, including bookkeeping the two solvers legitimately maintain differently, so it is all-or-nothing and cannot express "agrees to within a micron" — which is why the tolerance-based positional check is still the right CPU↔GPU criterion to build.

**The dominant real divergence: the GPU never had the CPU's adaptive iteration budget.** `Solver.cppm` runs `for (it = 0; it < max(iterations, max_iterations); ++it)` — a minimum of `iterations`, a ceiling of `max_iterations` (40), with two early exits: converged (`linear < convergence_threshold_linear && angular < convergence_threshold_angular`) and stalled (`linear > prev*0.99 && angular > prev*0.99`). `GpuSolver.cpp` looped exactly `m_solver_cfg.iterations` — **4 by default** — and never read `min_iterations`/`max_iterations`. Measured with matched counts on `stack`: CPU vs GPU(4) **3.523 mm**, CPU vs GPU(40) **0.860 mm**, CPU(40) vs GPU(40) **0.319 mm**; on `pyramid`, CPU vs GPU(4) **8.093 mm** → CPU vs GPU(40) **2.562 mm**. So the iteration budget is the dominant term and the residual after matching it is a few hundred microns. Three other suspects were checked first and are **faithful ports — do not re-audit them**: the dynamic speculative margin (`clamp(closing_speed·sub_dt·2, floor, ceiling)`, identical formula and identical closing-speed branch on both sides), the mass-scaled penalty floor (`contact_effective_mass/h²` clamped to `[base_floor, penalty_max]` with the same `active_normal` thresholds), and the adaptive warm-start `penalty = clamp(penalty·gamma, row_floor, penalty_max)`.

**The fix, and the dead machinery it revived.** The GPU already had convergence plumbing — `vbd_solve_color`, `vbd_update_lambda`, `vbd_apply_jacobi`, and `vbd_update_joint_lambda` all early-out on `state_converged_flag_index`, `vbd_derive_velocities` clears it per substep, and the per-iteration max violation is accumulated by `InterlockedMax` and zeroed by the last colour of each iteration — but **nothing ever set the flag**: it was wired and dead, so every early-out was unreachable. Added `vbd_convergence_check.slang`, a single-thread dispatch after each iteration's dual update that implements the CPU's two break conditions exactly, plus `state_convergence_prev_linear_index`/`state_convergence_prev_angular_index` (slots 15/16, below `state_pass_hash_base_index` so `collision_reset` zeroes them every substep — which is precisely the CPU's per-substep `previous_violation` lifetime, with 0 read as "no previous" standing in for the CPU's initial `float::max`), and the host loop now records `max(iterations, max_iterations)` iterations so the early-outs can retire the surplus. Ordering is safe because `derive_velocities` clears the flag after the iteration loop and before post-stabilize, so the post-stabilize pass — which shares `solve_color_pipeline` and its early-out — still runs. **Cost, measured: a uniform ~1.6–2.3× GPU slowdown, and it is real work rather than overhead — do not try to optimise it away the obvious way.** Every scene and both backends move together (drop VK 6.04 → 11.93 ms, stack DX12 3.75 → 7.62, mound VK 7.10 → 13.50, stress DX12 13.44 → 23.18, stress VK 14.12 → 26.89). The obvious cure was tried and **failed, and is recorded here as a negative result**: since the colour dispatches and the dual update already run through `indirect_args`, the convergence check was made to zero those workgroup counts when it raises the flag, so surplus iterations would launch nothing. It produced **no measurable speedup at all** (stack VK 11.94 → 12.02 ms, mound VK 13.50 → 13.63) **and it changed results** (stack VK `0xf751496f62d39a91` → `0x262a84fc4091dd6a`), so it was reverted and the hash returned to `0xf751496f62d39a91`. The null result is the informative part: if retiring the surplus iterations costs nothing, the surplus iterations were not being retired — **these scenes do not converge early, so the GPU genuinely executes the full budget, and so does the CPU.** That reframes the ~2× as the honest price of matching the CPU's real solver work; the old speed came from the GPU silently under-solving at a tenth of the iterations. The practical consequence is a good one: `Physics.solver_iterations` and `Physics.max_iterations` are now a **shared, parity-preserving throughput knob** — lowering the ceiling slows both solvers' convergence together and keeps them in agreement, where before the same setting moved only the CPU and silently widened the gap. If the surplus ever does need retiring on a scene that *does* converge early, profile first to confirm the early-out is actually being reached before touching the indirect args again.

**Result, measured on the same ladder immediately after (final-frame CPU↔GPU drift, Vulkan / DX12):**

| scene | before | after | gain |
|---|---|---|---|
| drop | 0.000000 / 0.000000 m | 0.000000 / 0.000000 m | already exact |
| pair | 0.000000 / 0.000000 m | 0.000000 / 0.000000 m | already exact |
| **stack** | 0.004190 / 0.004190 m | **0.000465 / 0.000465 m** | **9×** |
| **pyramid** | 0.008093 / 0.008086 m | **0.001722 / 0.001736 m** | **4.7×** |
| cluster | 0.0246 / 0.0247 m | 0.0320 / 0.0324 m | flat (bodies over 10 mm: 10→3, 6→4) |
| heap | 0.0304 / 0.0497 m | 0.0338 / 0.0360 m | flat (30→12, 24→11) |
| **mound** | 3.116 / 4.074 m | **0.373 / 0.373 m** | **8–11×** |
| **pile** | 3.745 / 3.268 m | **0.251 / 0.151 m** | **15–22×** |
| overlap | 9.485 / 13.258 m | 7.928 / 10.815 m | 1.2× |

The settling scenes are the headline: `stack` is now **sub-millimetre** and `pyramid` under 2 mm, against a floor of 0.319 mm measured when both solvers were pinned to 40 iterations — so the adaptive port lands essentially at the achievable limit, and the residual is the ordering/FMA noise that no iteration count can remove. Two readings worth keeping. First, the chaotic scenes improved *far* more than their headline drift suggests: `mound` and `pile` fell by an order of magnitude and the count of bodies past the 10 mm threshold roughly halved everywhere (cluster 10→3, heap 30→12, pile 125→63), which is the honest metric there — trajectories now track for much longer before Lyapunov separation takes over, exactly what closing a systematic solver-semantics gap should do. Second, `cluster` and `heap` final drift moved *up* slightly while their over-threshold counts fell sharply; that is the two-regimes rule in action and is not a regression — on a chaotic scene a single body's final position is a coin flip, so per-body distance must never be the gate criterion there. **`overlap` barely moved (1.2×), which is consistent with it being in the open nondeterminism class alongside `stress` and DX12 `pile`: its dominant error is not the iteration budget, and it should be hunted separately.**

### OPEN: the domino cascade dies on GPU — a distinct defect, and two hypotheses already refuted (2026-08-14)

**Reported from the Sandbox by eye, then confirmed and quantified.** `physics_stress` contains a 12-domino chain (0.3 × 2 × 1 m boxes, 30 kg, 0.9 m spacing, the first tilted −0.8 rad to start it). Measuring final centre height per domino (a 2 m box standing has y ≈ 1.0, toppled ≈ 0.4): **CPU topples 10/10; GPU topples 3/10.** The wave decays smoothly rather than stopping abruptly — GPU final heights run 0.384, 0.540, 0.669, 0.786, 0.923, 1.003, then 0.999 for the rest — and the tail is untouched, not merely unmoved: **dominos 8, 9 and 10 record a max speed of exactly 0.000 over all 600 frames.** The CPU's first-motion frames (2, 3, 7, 11, 44, 72, 102, 133, 154, 177) show the clean propagating wave the GPU never completes. Two candidate causes were tested directly and **both are refuted, so do not spend time on them again**: (1) *sleeping dominos acting as immovable walls that absorb the impact* — running the GPU with `Physics.velocity_sleep_threshold=0` so nothing ever sleeps made it **worse**, 2/10; (2) *convergence starvation* — the CPU still topples **10/10 when forced down to 4 iterations**, so the cascade is not convergence-limited at all, and raising the GPU to `max_iterations=120` made it **worse still, 1/10**. That last result is the sharpest clue available: on this scene **more solver iterations actively destroy the cascade on the GPU**, which points at energy being drained during impact resolution rather than at an under-solved contact — the opposite of the stack/pyramid story, where more iterations was exactly the fix. **Investigation round 2 (2026-08-14) — the cascade is entangled with the scene's nondeterminism, and that changes the order of work.** Repeat runs settle it: **CPU topples 11/11 twice, GPU topples 1/11, 4/11, 1/11.** So the failure is both *worse* than the first single-run sample suggested and *not a fixed quantity* — `stress` is already the standing member of the open nondeterminism class, and the domino count varies with it. Two instrument traps were paid for on the way and are worth recording. First, the GPU `contact_trace` reads a **capped contact dump**: at 512 entries it silently showed only ground contacts for the traced dominos, which nearly supported a false conclusion that the neighbour contact never forms; raising the cap to 4096 kept the picture qualitatively the same for the traced run but the scene still overflows it (4112 contacts), so **any single-run contact trace of `stress` is unreliable by construction**. Second, and more important, a trace taken from one run was compared against a state dump from another and they **disagreed about the same body** — the dump had domino 6 moving at frame 34 with peak speed 0.922 m/s while the trace showed it frozen after settling with only ground contacts. That is not a physics finding, it is two samples of a nondeterministic process, and it invalidates cross-run tracing on this scene. The methodological consequence is firm: **fix the `stress` nondeterminism before chasing the cascade**, because until then every measurement is a moving target and any "fix" can be confirmed or refuted by luck alone. The CPU's own stability (11/11 twice, and 10/10 even when starved to 4 iterations) means the reference behaviour is unambiguous and will still be there once the GPU scene is repeatable. Suspects to open next, in order: the restitution pass (GPU applies it through the compact-then-serialize path, and a domino chain is a sequence of single-point impacts, the regime that path is most likely to mis-order), then the angular response at a tipping edge contact (a 0.3 m-thin, 2 m-tall box topples almost entirely through angular momentum, so a damped or mis-scaled angular update would translate the box instead of rotating it, which matches the intermediate leaning heights), then friction/sticking at the base. Note this scene is `stress`, already the standing member of the open nondeterminism class alongside `overlap` — it is plausible, but not yet shown, that the cascade failure and the run-to-run variance share a cause. **This is a separate defect from the adaptive-iteration divergence fixed above; the ladder never caught it because every ladder scene measures a settled pile, and nothing on the ladder tests momentum propagating through a chain.** That gap is itself worth closing with a dedicated `ParityDomino` rung whose metric is toppled-count and wave-arrival frame, not final drift.

### `stress` GPU nondeterminism, localised to a single frame and a single cluster (2026-08-14)

**Run it with `--engine-bench-warmup-frames 0` or you will measure the wrong thing.** At the default 120-frame warmup two GPU runs appear to differ at recorded frame 0 across 886 of 1077 bodies by centimetres, which looks like a setup or spawn-order bug; that is entirely an artefact of the divergence having amplified unobserved during warmup. With warmup disabled the picture is clean: **frames 0–71 are bit-identical between runs, and at frame 72 exactly 44 of 1077 bodies diverge by a maximum of 1.6 × 10⁻⁵ m** before chaos amplifies it into the metre-scale spread and the 1-vs-4-of-11 domino outcomes. CPU runs of the same workload are identical at every frame.

**Two hypotheses tested and refuted on the way.** First, *variable spawn timing*: `build_stress_workload` gates on `wait_settled`, which resolves to `scheduler::all_settled()` — system-startup readiness (every system initialised and run once), **not** physics settling — and the GPU config compiles ~20 Slang shaders at runtime, so it was a plausible source of run-to-run drift. Measured directly, the spawn is frame-exact in both runs (frame 0 holds 1 body, frame 1 jumps to 1062, frame 2 to 1077), so **spawn timing is deterministic and is not the cause**. Second, *joints*: `stress` is the only gate scene that spawns joints, and this document already flags two unfixed joint hazards (the colouring conflict scan walks contacts only, so joint-connected bodies can share a colour; and islands run concurrently with no ordering on shared bodies), which made joints the leading suspect. The diverging set does not support it — the 44 bodies form **one spatially tight cluster** (x 10.31–12.31, y 4.25–7.70, z 24.93–28.95, in a scene spanning x −30.70–30.00), airborne and moving faster than the scene average (2.42 vs 1.57 m/s) — the shape of a dense colliding clump mid-fall, not of a joint chain. Joints are not cleared, but they are no longer the leading hypothesis.

**Next action, and the instrument is already built.** The per-pass GPU state hashes from the mound campaign exist precisely for this question: run two traced `stress` runs with warmup 0 and diff the pass-hash line at gen 72 — the first differing slot names the racing pass directly, exactly as it did for the grid/coloring hunt. Two cautions carry over from that campaign and from today: the `contact_trace` contact dump is capped (`debug_contact_dump_count`, raised 512 → 4096 today) and `stress` still overflows it at 4112 contacts, so per-contact lines on this scene are incomplete by construction — but the **pass hashes are computed on the GPU over the whole buffer and are unaffected by the dump cap**, which is what makes them the right tool here. And never compare a trace from one run against a dump from another on this scene until it is deterministic.

### The origin is the grid build's atomic ordering — and the first answer here was wrong (2026-08-14)

**Read the correction before the measurement, because the measurement below is sound and the conclusion first drawn from it was not.** The pass-hash diff named `s1[4]`, the substep-1 solve, as the first differing stage, and that reading was recorded here as "the racing pass". **It is retracted.** The pass hash combined each buffer slot's contribution with **XOR and never mixed the slot index**, so it was blind to *ordering*: it compared sets, not sequences. `collision_grid_build` allocates entries with `InterlockedAdd` and prepends into each bucket's linked list with a `InterlockedCompareExchange` loop, so identical input yields a **permuted** grid, and that permutation propagates to broad-phase traversal order, `collision_pairs`, `contact_data`, and each body's contact adjacency list — none of which the old hash could see. The solve was simply **the first order-sensitive consumer**: Gauss-Seidel sums contact gradients in buffer order and floating-point addition is not associative. Making the hash order-sensitive (mixing `ci`/`pi`/`walked`/`k` in nonlinearly, so index and content cannot factor back out of the XOR) moved the answer decisively, and the corrected picture is at the end of this section. **The general lesson is worth more than the finding: an order-blind instrument pointed at an ordering bug will confidently name the wrong pass — it names the first consumer that is order-sensitive.**

**The measurement.** Four traced Vulkan `stress` runs at `--engine-bench-warmup-frames 0`, plus two more with `Physics.use_jacobi=true`, all six otherwise flag-identical, diffed positionally on the per-pass hash line. The result is unusually clean. **In all four Gauss-Seidel runs the first differing generation is gen 64 — the same gen every time — and the first differing slot in chronological order is always `s1[4]`, the solve stage of substep 1.**

Everything upstream of it is bit-identical in every run: `entry`, `reentry`, `entryalt` and `bodyentry` (so the tick begins from identical state in *both* body buffers, which also rules out a stale or torn entry read), `pregrid` and `regrid`, `post-grid`, `post-broad`, `post-narrow`, `colors`/`recolors`/`colors2`, `adj`/`readj`, `warm0`/`warm1`, **the whole of substep 0 including its own solve**, and substep 1 through `post-predict` and `freeze`. Everything downstream inherits the difference and nothing else contributes one: `derive`, `restitution`, `stabilize`, `finalize`, and then `srcbody`/`dstbody`. Note that `srcbody == dstbody` within each run while differing across runs — **the cross-slot body copy is faithful and is copying already-diverged state, so it is not a torn copy**, which retires the obvious readback suspicion for this defect.

The four runs produced **three distinct** `s1[4]` values (`1097adc3` twice, `1ba2ca5f`, `1ecda26a`). More than two outcomes means a genuine scheduling race, not a bistable branch.

**The discriminator, and what it exonerates.** `Physics.use_jacobi=true` is **bit-identical across all 116 generations, every slot**. That is a real experiment rather than a degenerate one: Jacobi simulated visibly different physics (at gen 64 it holds 2608 contacts against Gauss-Seidel's 3109, and the traced body sits at −2.599902 rather than −2.599859), so the solver was doing full work and still reproduced itself exactly. Because `use_jacobi` short-circuits ahead of the `joint_count > 0` branch in `GpuSolver.cpp` and ahead of the `color_offset == 0xFFFFFFFF` branch in the shader, it bypasses **both** the per-colour dispatches and the island dispatch while still running `update_lambda`, `update_joint_lambda`, `vbd_convergence_check`, `derive_velocities`, restitution, post-stabilize and finalize. All of those therefore **reproduce deterministically and are exonerated** — in particular the newly added convergence check and its `InterlockedMax` accumulators, which were the obvious suspect as the freshest code in the pass. **The race is inside `vbd_solve_color`'s Gauss-Seidel paths.**

**A hypothesis raised and refuted the same hour, recorded so nobody re-runs it.** The obvious mechanism inside the island dispatch was a cross-island neighbour read: `solve_body` writes only `body_data[bi]` but reads `body_data[other_idx]` for every contact neighbour, and islands (union-find components of the *joint* graph) are disjoint in membership but seemingly not in contact, so island A's lane would read a body island B is writing. **This cannot happen.** `collision_broad_phase.slang` computes `cross_env = (env_i != 0xFFFFFFFF) && (env_j != 0xFFFFFFFF) && (env_i != env_j)` and refuses to emit the pair, so **two bodies in different islands never form a contact at all**. Islands touch only jointless bodies (written earlier, in separate ordered dispatches) and statics (never written). The island dispatch is not the origin. Also not implicated, and dormant on this scene: the `env == 0` sequential colour sweep inside the island dispatch, since `jointless_body_count > 0` sets `color_count = 0` so `seq_colors` evaluates to 0.

**The corrected root cause, with a controlled bracket around it.** `pregrid` and `regrid` are dispatched at `GpuSolver.cpp:1550` and `:1577`, and between them the **only** dispatch that executes is `collision_grid_build` at `:1558` — so they form a controlled bracket around exactly one pass. Under the order-sensitive hash, two Gauss-Seidel runs give **`pregrid` identical and `regrid` different at gen 3, the very first traced generation**, while the order-blind hash saw nothing there at all. **The grid build alone introduces the difference, and the difference is purely ordering.** The mechanism is plain in `collision_grid_build.slang`: `InterlockedAdd(grid_data[0], 1u, slot)` hands out entry slots in atomic arrival order, and a `InterlockedCompareExchange` loop prepends each entry into its bucket's linked list, so bucket order is arrival order. Neither has any ordering guarantee; identical input legitimately produces a permuted grid.

**The full chain, and the numbers that separate ordering from content.** Order-sensitive `regrid` diverges at **gen 3**; the pre-grid entry state follows at **gen 5** (the permutation has fed back through persisting pairs/contacts and warm starts); the **order-insensitive bodies-only hash `bodyentry` — which indexes `body_data` positionally and so cannot see ordering — first differs at gen 71**, `srcbody` at 71, and the traced body's own printed state at **73**. That is the honest statement of the defect: **the buffers are permuted from the third generation onward, the physics stays bit-identical for roughly seventy generations anyway, and somewhere around gen 71 the non-associative floating-point sum over a permuted contact list finally rounds differently** — which is precisely the frame-72 onset the state dump reported, and which chaos then amplifies to the metre scale and the 1-vs-4-of-11 domino outcomes.

**The causality test was run, and the answer is no — fixing the grid does not fix `stress`.** A throwaway deterministic grid build (single-threaded, bodies in index order, cost ignored) was dropped in and the pair re-run. It works as intended and the isolation is exact: counting generations where `pregrid` matches but `regrid` differs — the signature of the grid build *introducing* a difference — gives **2 with the racy grid (gens 3 and 4, the only generations where `pregrid` was still clean) and 0 with the deterministic grid**. So the grid build was genuinely a source, and it is genuinely eliminated. **The divergence survived anyway**, and the first differing stage moved one pass down the chain to **`s0[9]`, the broad phase**, at the same gen 3. The mechanism is identical in kind: `emit_pair` does `InterlockedAdd(collision_pairs[0], 1, slot)`, one thread per body, all appending concurrently, so **pair order is atomic arrival order and a deterministic grid traversal does not constrain it at all**. Narrow-phase contact emission and the colour lists are built the same way and are next in line behind it.

**So the defect is a class, not a pass, and that is the finding that matters.** Every buffer in the collision pipeline — grid entries, collision pairs, contacts, colour lists — is filled by a lock-free atomic append, so **none of them has any ordering guarantee**, while the Gauss-Seidel solve consumes contacts in buffer order where floating-point addition is not associative. Fixing one stage simply promotes the next one to first-differing. A worthwhile cross-check on that reading: with the deterministic grid the *content* divergence appeared **earlier**, not later (order-insensitive `bodyentry` at gen 53 against gen 71 before, traced body state at 56 against 73). That is not a regression — it is a different permutation amplifying at a different rate, and it is direct evidence that the grid was not even the dominant contributor. **Any fix must make the whole pipeline order-stable, or make the solve order-insensitive; a per-pass fix will keep moving the symptom.** The throwaway has been reverted; the order-sensitive hash is kept.

**Where it actually bites: the per-body adjacency sort has ties.** The pipeline already carries the designed defence against all of this — `collision_build_adjacency.slang:149-171` insertion-sorts each body's contact list by `(other_body, sbi-side local anchor x/y/z, feature_key)`, which is precisely an attempt to make the solve's walk order independent of the racy scatter above it. **It is not achieving that.** The `adj`/`readj` hash could not see the failure because it hashed only `body_a`/`body_b` and never mixed the list position `k` — a *set* check wearing an order check's name. Rebuilt to be order-sensitive and content-keyed (anchors, `feature_key`, position `k`, mixed nonlinearly, still never hashing `ci` so it stays immune to contact-index permutation), it says: **the adjacency walk order differs between runs from gen 3**, while `adj == readj` within every run in every generation — so it is a genuinely different order, not a torn read. Two structural explanations were checked and eliminated: the scatter/sort barrier **is** present (`:147`), and the shader is dispatched as **one workgroup** (`GpuSolver.cpp:1651`, `vec3u{1,1,1}`), so `AllMemoryBarrierWithGroupSync` does synchronize everything it needs to. **What remains is ties.** The sort is stable and its input is the nondeterministic `InterlockedAdd` scatter, so any pair of contacts equal on the key keeps arrival order — and the key omits the contact normal, the *other* side's anchor, and `c0`, so two contacts that differ physically can compare equal. **The fix that follows, and doubles as the test: extend the sort key to cover the rest of the contact content, so a tie can only occur between contacts that are genuinely interchangeable.** It is shader-only and the rebuilt `adj` hash validates it directly. Note it changes contact processing order, so it changes results and re-baselines every GPU hash in the gate — unlike the instrument changes, this one is a solver-behaviour change.

**The tie fix was built, measured, and FAILED — and the failure narrows the hunt usefully.** The sort key was extended to a guaranteed total order over the whole contact identity (other body, both anchors bitwise via `asuint`, `feature_key` hi/lo, normal, `c0`), which is NaN-safe and cannot tie except on genuinely identical contacts. **It changed nothing**: `adj` still differed run-to-run in 116 of 116 generations, first at gen 3, exactly as before. The shader really was live (`collision_build_adjacency` recompiles at runtime, confirmed in the log). **So ties are refuted too, and the change was reverted** — it alters contact processing order and re-baselines the gate for zero measured benefit.

**What the failure proves, by elimination.** A total order over identical contents uniquely determines the sorted sequence. So if the sorted sequence still differs, either the sort is not what orders the array, or **the per-body multiset being sorted is not the same in both runs**. An A/B on the same hash settles which: with position `k` mixed in it differs from **gen 3**; with `k` removed and everything else identical — an order-blind multiset check over the same fields — it differs only from **gen 40**. So for generations 3 to 39 the multiset matched while the order did not, which pointed at the sort. But hashing **exactly the sort key, role-normalized, plus position** still differs from **gen 4**. Those two results together mean the earlier order-blind check was matching on a *partial* field set (it covered only `body_a`/`body_b`, `local_anchor_a.x`, `local_anchor_b.x`, `feature_key`) and the fuller key does not match — i.e. **the per-body contact multiset genuinely differs between runs from gen 3-4 in the fields that partial check never looked at: anchor y/z, the opposite-side anchor, the normal, and `c0`.**

**That reframes the defect and is where the next session should start.** If the contact *content* differs while body state is still bit-identical, the problem is not merely that buffers are permuted — **the narrow phase is producing different contact geometry for the same input depending on scheduling**, and ordering fixes anywhere downstream cannot help. The next measurement is therefore a per-pair content check inside the narrow phase, not another ordering instrument: for a fixed body pair at gen 3-4, dump the generated manifold in both runs and diff normal / anchors / `c0` / `feature_key`. Candidate mechanisms to look at first, none tested: manifold reduction that keeps N points with ties broken by insertion order, reference-face selection with a tie, and any accumulation into a contact that more than one thread can reach.

### The colouring is CLEAN — a "root cause" claimed and retracted the same hour (2026-08-14)

**Retraction, in full.** A scan was added counting pairs of bodies that share both a colour and a contact, and it reported conflicts in **92 of 116 generations from gen 10**, stable on re-read. That was written up here as the root cause: same-colour contact pairs are solved concurrently by `vbd_solve_color`, and `solve_body` writes `body_data[bi]` while reading `body_data[other_idx]`, so they would race. **The measurement was invalid and the conclusion is withdrawn.** The scan lived in `vbd_hash_adjacency`, whose two dispatches (`adj`, `readj`) are recorded at `GpuSolver.cpp:1659` and `:1666` — **before** the `collision_build_coloring` dispatch at `:1675`. It was therefore comparing the **previous substep's `color_data`** against the **current** contact adjacency, which manufactures conflicts out of nothing.

**What the correct measurements say — the colouring is right, and this is now closed.** Two independent checks, both taken at the correct point:

- The colouring's **own** counters, computed inside the shader against the authoritative `gs_live_colors` and published to `state_coloring_fallback_index` / `state_coloring_conflict_index`: **zero conflicts and zero fallback in every one of 116 generations.** The fallback at `:187-195` never fires on this scene at all.
- A re-sited conflict scan moved into `vbd_hash_colors` (dispatched *after* the colouring) reading the fresh `color_data`: **zero same-colour contact pairs at all three colouring hash points, all 116 generations.** This covers the flatten as well as the assignment, which the shader's own check does not.

So the colouring produces a valid assignment *and* flattens it faithfully. The algorithm already implements a correct Jones–Plassmann priority rule (`body_priority`, with an index tiebreak), so the "concurrent-commit race in the colouring" hypothesis is refuted too.

**The two constant sweeps were therefore measuring nothing, and one of them was invalid anyway.** `max_colors` 16 → 48 and `coloring_rounds` 32 → 256 both left the (bogus) conflict count unchanged; both are reverted. Independently of that, **`max_colors` can never exceed 32**, because `neighbor_colors` is a `uint` bitmask and `1u << other_color` wraps for `other_color >= 32` — so the 48 test could not have worked even against a real defect. Worth fixing or asserting if anyone raises `max_colors` in future. (Also: the first `coloring_rounds` test silently ran a **stale binary** because the build hit the known `.gcm` "File exists" holder. Always check the ninja tail before believing a null result.)

**A fix was built on the retracted diagnosis and has been fully reverted**: reserving `max_colors - 1` as a serially-solved overflow bucket, routing the fallback into it, and solving it from a single thread. It is not needed — the fallback never fires — and it changed physics for nothing. `collision_build_coloring.slang` and `vbd_solve_color.slang` are back to their pre-session state.

**The methodological lesson, which is the durable part.** Three wrong answers in one session — "the racing pass is `s1[4]` solve", "islands race through a shared neighbour", "the colouring emits conflicts" — and **every one came from an instrument that was measuring the right quantity at the wrong place**: an order-blind hash pointed at an ordering bug, a scan that walked static bodies the sort deliberately skips, and a scan that read a buffer one dispatch before it was written. Before trusting any pass-hash-derived claim, check **where in the substep the hash dispatch actually sits** relative to the pass it is supposed to describe, and prefer a counter computed *inside* the pass being judged over one computed from its outputs elsewhere.

**What is now established about everything downstream of it, so nobody re-audits these.** The per-dynamic-body contact adjacency the solve walks is **canonically ordered and deterministic**: a direct check for ordering violations and key ties under the sort's own comparison reports **zero of both in all 116 generations**. The earlier claim that adjacency order diverged was **contaminated by static bodies** — the sort deliberately skips locked bodies (`if (body_data[sbi].locked != 0) continue`) and statics are never solved, so their unsorted lists are irrelevant; once the check applies the same filter, the order is clean. Contact content (normal, `c0`) is identical to gen ~49, body content to gen ~65, and colours only diverge at ~66, i.e. **after** bodies, confirming the colour divergence is a consequence rather than a cause.

**The fix, and the trap in the cheap version.** Give the colouring a deterministic priority rule so two contacting bodies can never commit the same colour in the same round, and make the fallback safe rather than silent — a body that genuinely cannot be coloured must go to a serialized bucket (the island dispatch already demonstrates the pattern) instead of being dropped into a colour it conflicts with. Do **not** "fix" this by raising `max_colors` or `coloring_rounds`; both were measured and neither moves the number. The conflict scan built for this investigation should become a permanent gate check — a same-colour contact pair is a correctness invariant violation, and it was sitting in the code counted-but-tolerated (`gs_coloring_conflicts`, `gs_coloring_fallback`) while an earlier note in this document dismissed it as "real but ~1.7/frame and doesn't correlate with the blowup". That dismissal was answering a different question: the conflicts never explained the *blowup*, but they are the whole explanation for the *nondeterminism*.

### The colour partition is exact, and the pass axis is now exhausted (2026-08-14)

**Last structural hypothesis, closed.** Every conflict scan up to this point looked for two *different* contacting bodies sharing a colour; none checked whether the same body index appears twice. A duplicate would put two threads on the same `body_data[bi]` with no contact relationship at all, invisible to every earlier check. Scanned post-colouring, per non-locked body, counting its occurrences across all colour lists: **bodies appearing in more than one colour slot = 0 of 86 generations; non-locked bodies appearing in no colour = 0 of 86.** `color_data` is an exact partition of the dynamic bodies. Also confirmed the solve path uses **no wave intrinsics and no groupshared** (`vbd_solve_color`, `vbd_update_lambda`, `vbd_derive_velocities`), so wave composition and occupancy cannot influence results.

**The contradiction, stated plainly because it is the useful output.** At the divergence generation the substep-1 solve provably receives identical bodies *and* identical contacts (the original order-blind composite hash matches through `s1[3] freeze`), canonically-ordered adjacency (zero sort violations, zero key ties), a valid conflict-free colouring that exactly partitions the bodies, an identical iteration budget, no wave intrinsics, no groupshared, and demonstrably sufficient barriers (giving every colour its own pass changed nothing). **And it still produces different bodies.** Every mechanism identifiable from the shader source has been eliminated by measurement, which means one of the "verified" premises is false in a way the current instruments cannot see.

**The consequence for whoever picks this up: stop eliminating mechanisms and change the axis of attack.** The pass axis is exhausted — a dozen mechanisms tested, all clean. The untried axis is *which body*. Use the instrument that actually found this defect (`--engine-bench-state-dump-out` plus the comparator, which never touches the pass-hash path) to name the first body that diverges and the exact frame, then trace that single body's contacts, colour, island membership and neighbours. Localising by body is independent of everything tried so far, and it is how the campaign's earlier wins were made — the half-gravity and warm-start-lineage bugs both fell to per-body traces, not per-pass hashes.

### The readback is SOUND — a "torn readback" scare raised and retracted within the hour (2026-08-14)

**What was claimed.** `vbd_hash_adjacency` was reduced to: thread 0 reads `collision_state[state_contact_count_index]` once and writes that single value into **two** pass-hash slots with consecutive stores. Those slots disagreed in **35 of 116 generations** (gen 10: `23` in one, `373` in the other). That was written up as proof the host snapshot of `collision_state` is torn, which would have invalidated every onset generation in this document.

**Two follow-up tests refute it, and the claim is withdrawn.**

- **Constant marker.** The same shader was changed to store a *literal constant* into the same two slots from the same thread. The slots agree in **0 of 56 generations** — i.e. never disagree. So the readback path, the slot arithmetic, and the single-thread double-store all work correctly. **The snapshot is not torn.**
- **The real pass.** `collision_build_adjacency` — which reads that same `state_contact_count_index` — was instrumented to record the count at entry and again at exit of its own dispatch. It is **identical in 76 of 76 generations**. No solver pass observes the count mutating underneath it.

**So the pass-hash onset numbers stand**, and the sweeping invalidation written here earlier is withdrawn. The likeliest explanation for the original disagreement is that the compiler rematerialised the load from a read-write buffer rather than keeping it in a register, so the two stores did not write the same register; whatever the mechanism, it is confined to a diagnostic shader and does not implicate the solver. It is not worth further time.

**The lesson is the same one this session keeps teaching, and it now cuts both ways.** Four false leads came from instruments measuring the right quantity in the wrong place; this one came from *over-correcting* — treating a diagnostic anomaly as proof of a systemic defect without first checking whether a real pass was affected. **Before promoting an instrument anomaly to an engine defect, reproduce it in a pass that actually matters.** A constant is the cheapest possible control and it should have been the first test, not the third.

### The convergence check is ruled out, and the open hypothesis is the indirect dispatch (2026-08-14)

**Iteration count measured, inside the pass that decides it.** `vbd_convergence_check` now records `pc.iteration + 1` into a spare pass-hash slot on each of its two exit paths (converged and stalled, distinguished by a `0x10000` marker), per substep — a counter written *inside* the judged pass rather than derived from its outputs. Result: the effective iteration count **first differs between runs at gen 69, while `bodyentry` (body content) first differs at gen 53**. The iteration count is therefore **downstream of the divergence, not its cause**, and the adaptive-budget machinery is exonerated on this scene. Most generations run the full budget on both substeps; the early exits that do fire are overwhelmingly the *stalled* branch, not the converged one.

**What that leaves, and the hypothesis now staged.** At the divergence generation the solve receives bit-identical bodies, bit-identical contact content, canonically-ordered adjacency, a valid conflict-free colouring, and an identical iteration budget — and still produces different bodies. The remaining difference is in **how the dispatches execute**, and there is one structural asymmetry that lines up with every result so far: the per-colour solve dispatches go through **`dispatch_indirect`**, while the Jacobi path — the one configuration that is bit-identical across all 116 generations — uses plain **`dispatch`**. If the automatic barrier derivation misses a UAV hazard on indirect dispatches, consecutive colour dispatches would overlap despite a perfectly valid colouring, producing exactly the observed signature: identical inputs, different output, immune to every ordering and colouring fix. It also fits the open nondeterminism class being the largest, densest scenes (`stress`, `overlap`, `pile`), where overlap has the most opportunity to manifest.

**The probe is staged in the working tree but has not been run**, because the build is blocked by unrelated in-progress work on the video encoder. In `GpuSolver.cpp` the jointless colour loop temporarily issues `rec.dispatch(body_workgroups, 1, 1)` instead of `rec.dispatch_indirect(f.jointless_indirect_dispatch_buffer, ...)`; this is correct rather than merely expedient, because the coloured path already guards with `if (idx < count)` — it simply launches more workgroups than the exact count. If `stress` becomes deterministic under it, the indirect path's barrier derivation is the defect; if it does not, indirect dispatch is eliminated and the hunt moves inside the island dispatch.

**Jacobi does not exonerate anything — that inference is also retracted.** Under the order-sensitive instrument, `Physics.use_jacobi=true` is identical across all 116 generations **including `regrid`**, so under Jacobi the grid build happens to come out deterministically too. Since the grid build is the same code receiving the same input in both modes, the difference is manifestation, not correctness: the Gauss-Seidel dispatch pattern (many indirect colour dispatches plus the island dispatch, versus two flat dispatches) changes occupancy and overlap enough to expose an ordering the code never guaranteed in the first place. **A configuration in which a latent race does not fire is not evidence that the race is elsewhere.** The earlier claim that Jacobi's determinism exonerated `update_lambda`, `vbd_convergence_check`, derive, restitution, stabilize and finalize is therefore withdrawn — they were never tested, they simply were not reached by a divergence that had not yet appeared.

### Localised by body: every seed is a rotating box, and the divergence is born in the ANGULAR state (2026-08-14)

**The pass axis was exhausted, so this round attacked the body axis, and it produced the sharpest localisation the campaign has.** Instrument: `--engine-bench-state-dump-out` on paired Vulkan `stress` runs at `--engine-bench-warmup-frames 0`, differenced **bitwise per field** (position / linear velocity / orientation / angular velocity separately) rather than through the comparator's scalar drift, which collapses all four into one distance and hides which channel moved first. One cheap trick makes the whole thing legible and should be reused: **scene names hash to owner ids with plain FNV-1a 64 (`gse::stable_id`), so hashing `Tumbler 1 Cube 293` and friends turns an anonymous dump into a named body list.** Every claim below is over three independent run pairs.

**Three pairs, three different seeds, and the seed is NOT always the same object — never conclude the site from one pair.** Pair A/B seeds at frame 60 in 50 bodies, all of them Tumbler 1 content cubes; pair E/F seeds at frame 65 in 18 bodies, all of them Tumbler 0 content cubes; pair C/D seeds at frame 91 in exactly **three bodies — Domino 1, 2 and 3**, mid-topple. Not one seed body in any pair came from anywhere else in the 1077-body scene. The onset frame moves the way the existing note says it does (60 / 65 / 91 here), and cross-pairings show why: A, C and D agree with each other far longer than any of them agrees with B, so each pair's onset is simply the earliest introduction event on which those two particular runs happen to differ.

**The finding that matters: the divergence is introduced in the ROTATIONAL state, not the translational one.** Counting how many seed bodies differ in each channel at the seed frame — E/F: orientation **18 of 18**, angular velocity 17 of 18, but position only **5 of 18** and never by more than **2 ULP**; C/D: orientation 3 of 3, angular velocity 3 of 3, position 2 of 3 at ≤ 2 ULP; A/B (a larger, already-amplified event): orientation 50 of 50, angular velocity 50 of 50, position 34 of 50. **Orientation diverges in 100% of seed bodies in all three events while position is bit-identical in most of them.** The magnitudes say the same thing: in the C/D seed the position deltas are 1–2 ULP while `wy` — the near-zero *off-axis* angular velocity of a domino toppling about z — moves from −8.24e-5 to −1.01e-4, a ~20% relative change. That is the signature of **catastrophic cancellation in a sum of near-cancelling torque terms**, which is exactly what a per-body angular accumulation over a permuted contact list produces, and it explains why the linear channel can stay bit-identical while the angular one does not.

**Rotation, not speed and not contact density, predicts where the seed lands.** Ranking scene groups by peak angular speed over frames 40–90 almost exactly reproduces their divergence order (Tumbler1 35.8 rad/s → frame 60; Tumbler0 32.5 → 73; vise 23.5 → 74; cornerdrop 1.2 → 75), while **every group that moves fast but barely rotates never seeds at all**: `elevator` runs 100% in motion at 2.66 m/s mean for the whole run with peak angular speed 0.000 and stays bit-identical across 120 frames; `j.sphere` peaks at 9.8 m/s, `springs` at 8.6 m/s, both with zero angular speed, and neither seeds. Honest caveat: the two tumblers are 876 of 1077 bodies, so body count is a real confound for them — the domino seed (8 bodies, 0.7% of the scene) is the sample that argues against pure size, and it is one sample.

**The seed stays strictly local for ~13 frames and then the entire awake scene diverges in a single frame — that is a global coupling, and it is not physical.** In pair A/B, Tumbler 1 diverges alone from frame 60 to frame 72, growing 50 → 281 bodies with **zero leakage** to any other group. At frame 73 Tumbler 0 goes from 0 to **369 of 438 bodies at once**, and the funnel, dominoes, joint chain, inverted pyramid and slope all break in that same frame. Tumbler 0 is 24 m away with no contact path to Tumbler 1, so nothing physical carries that. What is left is a scene-wide reduction, and the obvious channel is the convergence check's `InterlockedMax` accumulators and the iteration budget derived from them: every body's iteration count depends on a max over all bodies, so one perturbed body eventually flips the budget for everything. **This does not contradict "iteration count first differs at gen 69 while bodyentry differs at 53" — it explains it.** The iteration count is downstream of the seed, and it is also the amplifier that globalises it, which is why the lag is ~13–16 generations in both measurements. The groups that never diverge (`boxgrid` 90 bodies, `impact`, `spherestack`, `elevator`, `j.hinge`, `j.fixed`) are the settled or non-rotating ones — a sleeping body is not solved, so the global flip cannot reach it.

**A traced seed-adjacent body says the narrow phase was NOT producing different contact geometry, and that partially retracts the previous round's reframe.** `ContactTrace.body=9891601923133506532` (`Domino 3`) on both runs of a pair, keyed per contact by `(body_a, body_b, feature_key)` so the key is immune to contact-index permutation: the contact **index `ci` differs from gen 3** (indices 129–132 and 2007 in one run against 27–30 and 1104 in the other), while the contact **set and its geometry — `c0`, normal, `ra`, `rb`, feature keys — are bit-identical through gen 68**, and contact content and body state then first differ **at the same generation, 69**. So for this body the narrow phase consumed permuted buffers for sixty-six generations and emitted identical geometry, and the contact content moved only after the body did. The earlier statement that "the per-body contact multiset genuinely differs from gen 3-4" was an **inference** from hashes that all included the list position `k`; no full-content, position-free hash was ever taken. It may still be true for *some* bodies, but it is not what happens at this one, and it is not a licence to stop looking at ordering.

**Eliminated this round.** Contact-buffer truncation as a content-difference source: `max_contacts` is **262144** against a measured peak of **3710** — 71× headroom, `dropped 0`, `dup 0`, and `fallback 0 conflicts 0` confirming the colouring result independently. The `ci >= max_contacts` drop path in `collision_narrow_phase` never fires on this scene.

**Two corrections to standing notes in this document.** First, "the solve path uses no wave intrinsics" is true of `vbd_solve_color` / `vbd_update_lambda` / `vbd_derive_velocities`, but **the narrow phase's contact allocator is wave-cooperative** — `collision_narrow_phase.slang:1446-1453` uses `WaveActiveSum` / `WavePrefixSum` / `WaveIsFirstLane` / `WaveReadLaneFirst` over an `InterlockedAdd` base, so which pair lands in which wave and lane, and therefore every contact's `ci`, is a function of the atomically-ordered `collision_pairs` list. The integer reductions themselves are exact, so this is a permutation source, not a content source — but it is the mechanism that permutes `ci`, and it should not be described as absent. Second, `warm_start_lookup` (`:1306-1334`) walks body `ba`'s **previous-frame** adjacency list and returns the **first** entry matching `(body_a, body_b, feature_key)`, then `reuse_cached_sticking` (`:1516-1518`) **replaces the freshly computed anchors with the cached ones**. That is a real feedback path from adjacency order into contact geometry; it is unambiguous only while no two contacts of a pair share a feature key, and nobody has measured whether that holds.

**Where the next session should point its instrument.** The evidence now favours the plainest remaining explanation over any race: **the solve is deterministic given its inputs, but its per-body contact walk order is not, and the angular accumulation is the numerically fragile half of it.** The measurement that would settle it is a per-body angular-gradient check inside `solve_body` — accumulate the torque sum both in walk order and in a canonical order (or in a compensated sum) and count bodies where they differ — a counter computed *inside* the judged pass, on the body axis. If they differ, the fix is to make the per-body accumulation order-invariant rather than to keep chasing which buffer permuted. Do not re-attack this through the narrow phase without first taking a full-content, position-free contact hash, which has still never been measured.

### The angular-gradient counter, built and run — and it REFUTES the angular-fragility hypothesis while closing the causal chain (2026-08-14)

**The instrument.** Inside `solve_body` (`vbd_solve_color.slang`), alongside the existing `grad` / `ang_grad` accumulation and touching neither, three extra accumulators per channel sum the *identical term sequence* two ways: sequentially, and split by term parity into two partials that are added at the end. Same terms, same values, different association. After the contact loop, bodies with at least two terms compare the two sums bitwise and publish four counters plus two magnitudes through `InterlockedAdd` / `InterlockedMax` into tick-scope `collision_state` slots that nothing else writes; `collision_reset.slang` clears them at `pc.substep == 0`, because tick-scope fields are deliberately skipped by its zeroing loop. It is a counter computed **inside the pass being judged**, which is the rule this campaign keeps having to relearn. Slots reuse the dead tick-hash names, so in the `contact trace gen N pass hashes` line read them as **`adj` = angular-sensitive solves, `readj` = linear-sensitive solves, `colors` = solves checked, `recolors` = worst condition-scaled angular error, `colors2` = worst condition-scaled linear error**. Cost is negligible — `stress` p50 19.845 ms against a 19.596 ms baseline — so it does not invalidate timings the way the old coloring probe did, but it is a production-path branch and should not ride along on gate or timing rounds.

**One trap paid for immediately, and it is the general one.** The first version normalised by `length(seq)`, and reported a relative error of **1.0 in nearly every generation** — which reads as "the angular sum is completely different". It is an artefact: when the terms nearly cancel, `seq` is near zero, so dividing by it turns ordinary last-bit noise into a 100% relative error. **Normalise a cancellation-prone sum by the sum of term magnitudes, never by the result.** Changed to `length(alt - seq) / Σ|term|` the picture inverted completely.

**The result, and it kills the hypothesis that motivated the build.** Over 76 generations and 2,412,072 checked body-solves: **angular-sensitive 1,915,629 (79.4%), linear-sensitive 1,774,506 (73.6%)** — the same incidence within a few points. Worst condition-scaled error per generation, median across generations: **angular 1.81e-7, linear 2.25e-7, ratio 0.8×** — the angular sum is if anything *less* sensitive than the linear one, and both sit at one to two float ULP relative to the term scale. **There is no catastrophic cancellation in the angular accumulation. "The angular gradient is the numerically fragile half" is REFUTED — do not rebuild this hypothesis.**

**So the state dump's angular-first signature needs a different explanation, and the boring one is right.** Orientation is simply a **finer detector** than position: the quaternion is O(1) with a ULP of 6e-8, while position on this scene is O(10–30 m) with a ULP near 1e-6, so the same relative perturbation becomes visible in the quaternion several ULP before it moves a position bit. That fully accounts for "orientation diverges in 100% of seed bodies while position is bit-identical in most of them" with no angular-specific mechanism, and the earlier reading of that signature is withdrawn.

**What the counter does establish is the more valuable half, and it closes the chain.** The per-body contact-gradient sum is order-sensitive in **~79% of all body-solves, from gen 3 — the first traced generation**, which is exactly the generation at which contact indices and the per-body adjacency walk order were already measured to differ between runs (`adj` differs run-to-run 116 of 116 generations from gen 3, while `adj == readj` within each run, so it is a genuinely different order and not a torn read). Put the two measurements together and no race is required anywhere: **permuted contact order (measured) × an order-sensitive sum (now measured) ⇒ differently-rounded gradients for four fifths of all body solves from gen 3 onward**, and after roughly 4800 solves per body — 40 iterations × 2 substeps × ~60 frames — one of those differences finally crosses into a stored body float, which is the frame-60-to-91 onset the state dump reports. **This dissolves the standing contradiction rather than adding to it: the premise that was false was never "the solve receives identical contacts" — that one held — it was "the solve receives them in an identical ORDER", which nobody ever claimed and which was measured false at gen 3 a round ago.** Every mechanism eliminated on the pass axis was eliminated correctly; they were all the wrong kind of thing to look for, because the defect is not a race.

**Consequence for the fix.** Chasing which buffer permuted is futile — it was called a class, not a pass, and that reading is now confirmed from the consuming end. The two options that actually terminate are to make the per-body contact walk order canonical *and* stable across runs (the adjacency sort already tries this and demonstrably fails, so the failure is in its input multiset for some bodies, which is still unmeasured by a position-free full-content hash), or to make the accumulation itself order-invariant — fixed-point or compensated summation of the contact gradients, which costs a little arithmetic and buys exact reproducibility regardless of buffer order. Given that the permutation source is lock-free atomic append in four separate stages, the second is the one likely to survive contact with the rest of the pipeline.

### Order-invariant accumulation and the restitution tiebreak: two real defects fixed, `stress` still not deterministic (2026-08-14)

**Both changes are in the tree and both are shader-only. Neither achieves the goal yet, and the honest scoreboard is at the end of this section.**

**Change 1 — fixed-point contact-gradient accumulation.** `solve_body` now accumulates the five contact-loop accumulators (`grad`, `ang_grad`, `hess`, `ang_hess`, `hess_xt`) as `int64_t` fixed point at a scale of 2²², decoding back to float immediately after the contact loop. Integer addition is associative and commutative, so the sum no longer depends on the order in which the adjacency walk delivers contacts. Scale choice matters and is constrained from both ends: `penalty_max` is **1e9 N/m**, so Hessian entries can be large, and 2²² keeps `|sum|` valid to 2.2e12 with a resolution of 2.4e-7 — the resulting position error is ~1.6e-11 m, far below anything physical, while the accumulation itself becomes exact. Using a power-of-two scale keeps `v * fx_scale` exact in float32, so the only loss is one rounding per term. **Not covered, and still order-dependent: the joint loop** (`accumulate_joint_for_body` accumulates into the same variables in float over the atomically-built `joint_adjacency`) **and the motor path** — neither is exercised by the seeds found so far, but both should be converted before this is called complete.

**What it bought, measured.** The onset stopped wandering: three float pairs seeded at frames 60, 65 and 91 in three different objects, while all three fixed-point pairs seeded at **exactly frame 35**. Diffuse rounding accumulation as a divergence source is gone; what remained was localised to a single event. That event turned out to be an impact — the diverging cubes were in free-fall with angular speed **exactly 0.000000** for the preceding five frames, and frame 35 is the frame they land.

**Change 2 — the restitution sort key.** `vbd_apply_restitution` compacts restitution-eligible contacts with `InterlockedAdd` (atomic arrival order), sorts them with a bitonic network, then applies them **serially from thread 0**. The sort key was `(body_a, body_b, replayed)` only. A box-box manifold emits up to four contacts for the same body pair — the `Domino 3` trace shows exactly four `0|6|…` contacts differing only in feature key — so all four tie, and **a bitonic network is not stable**: their final order is decided by the arrival order that seeded it. Serial restitution application is order-dependent, so identical inputs produced different post-impact velocities. The key now carries a 24-bit hash of `feature_key` as a tertiary term, which is derived from geometry and was measured identical between runs; primary and secondary ordering are unchanged. **This is the pass this document nominated as suspect number one for the domino cascade, on the grounds that "a domino chain is a sequence of single-point impacts, the regime that path is most likely to mis-order" — that reasoning was correct.**

**What it bought, measured.** The bistable impact flip is gone. Worst delta at onset fell from **7.04e-3 m and 1.46 rad/s** to **5.96e-8 m — one ULP** in two of three pairs, and post-impact angular speeds that previously differed thirtyfold (0.04 against 1.18 rad/s) now agree to two decimals across runs.

**Exonerated while here.** The restitution overflow path at `:80-89` walks contacts in raw `ci` order from a single thread and would be nondeterministic if reached, but a counter on the queue depth reports a peak of **199 against a capacity of 2048** — it never fires on this scene. Not a defect today; still worth fixing before someone raises the contact count.
